according to the swizzle pattern, as described in the following\n\ntables. These swizzle patterns define the mapping of the 16-byte chunks along the swizzle width to subgroups of four banks.\n\nIt is of type\nCUtensorMapSwizzle\nand has four options: none, 32 bytes, 64 bytes and 128 bytes. Note that the shared memory box’s\n\ninner dimension must be less or equal to the span of the swizzle pattern.\n7.29.3.2.\nThe Swizzle Modes\n\nAs previously mentioned, there are four swizzle modes. The following tables show the different swizzle patterns, including the relation of the new\n\nshared memory indices. The tables define the mapping of the 16-byte chunks along the 128 bytes to eight subgroups of four banks.\nFigure 28\nAn Overview of TMA Swizzle Patterns\n\nConsiderations.\nWhen applying a TMA swizzle pattern, it is crucial to adhere to specific memory requirements:\nGlobal memory alignment:\nGlobal memory must be aligned to 128 bytes.\nShared memory alignment:\nFor simplicity shared memory should be aligned according to the number of bytes after which the swizzle pattern repeats. When the shared memory buffer is not aligned by the number of bytes by which the swizzle pattern repeats itself, there is an offset between the swizzle pattern and the shared memory.\n\nSee\ncomment\n, below.\nInner dimension:\nThe inner dimension of the shared memory block must meet the size requirements specified in table\nTable 10\n. If these\n\nrequirements are not met, the instruction is considered invalid. Additionally, if the swizzle width exceeds the inner dimension,\n\nensure that the shared memory is allocated to accommodate the full swizzle width.\nGranularity:\nThe granularity of swizzle mapping is fixed at 16 bytes. This means that data is organized and accessed in chunks\n\nof 16 bytes, which must be considered when planning memory layout and access patterns.\nSwizzle Pattern Pointer Offset Computation\n. Here, we describe how to determine the offset between the swizzle pattern and the shared memory, when the shared memory buffer is not aligned by the number of bytes by which the swizzle pattern repeats itself.\n\nWhen using TMA, the shared memory is required to be aligned to 128 bytes. of a Modified Tensor Map\n\nIn contrast to using a tensor map that is passed as a\nconst\n__grid_constant__\nkernel parameter, using a tensor map in\n\nglobal memory requires explicitly establishing a release-acquire pattern in the tensor map proxy between the threads\n\nthat modify the tensor map and the threads that use it.\nThe release part of the pattern was shown in the previous section. It is accomplished using\n\nthe\ncuda::ptx::tensormap.cp_fenceproxy\nfunction.\nThe acquire part is accomplished using the\ncuda::ptx::fence_proxy_tensormap_generic\nfunction that wraps the\nfence.proxy.tensormap::generic.acquire\ninstruction. If the two threads participating in the release-acquire pattern are on the same device, the\n.gpu\nscope suffices. If the threads are on\n\ndifferent devices, the\n.sys\nscope must be used. Once a tensor map has been acquired by one thread, it can be used by other threads in the block\n\nafter sufficient synchronization, for example, using\n__syncthreads()\n. The thread that uses the tensor map and the thread that performs the fence\n\nmust be in the same block. That is, if the threads are in, for example, two different thread blocks of the same cluster, the same grid, or a\n\ndifferent kernel, synchronization APIs such as\ncooperative_groups::cluster\nor\ngrid_group::sync()\nor stream-order synchronization do not\n\nsuffice to establish ordering for tensor map updates, that is, threads in these other thread blocks still need to acquire the tensor map proxy\n\nat the right scope before using the updated tensor map. If there are no intermediate modifications, the fence does not have to be repeated\n\nbefore each\ncp.async.bulk.tensor\ninstruction.\nThe\nfence\nand subsequent use of the tensor map is shown in the The remaining data cache serves as an L1 cache and is also used by the texture unit that implements the various addressing and data filtering modes mentioned in\nTexture and Surface Memory\n.\n16.8.2.\nGlobal Memory\n\nGlobal memory behaves the same way as for devices of compute capability 5.x (See\nGlobal Memory\n).\n16.8.3.\nShared Memory\n\nSimilar to the\nNVIDIA Ampere GPU architecture\n, the amount of the unified data cache reserved for shared memory is configurable on a per kernel basis. For the\nNVIDIA H100 Tensor Core GPU architecture\n, the unified data cache has a size of 256 KB for devices of compute capability 9.0. The shared memory capacity can be set to 0, 8, 16, 32, 64, 100, 132, 164, 196 or 228 KB.\nAs with the\nNVIDIA Ampere GPU architecture\n, an application can configure its preferred shared memory capacity, i.e., the\ncarveout\n. Devices of compute capability 9.0 allow a single thread block to address up to 227 KB of shared memory. Kernels relying on shared memory allocations over 48 KB per block are architecture-specific, and must use dynamic shared memory rather than statically sized shared memory arrays. These kernels require an explicit opt-in by using\ncudaFuncSetAttribute()\nto set the\ncudaFuncAttributeMaxDynamicSharedMemorySize\n; see\nShared Memory\nfor the Volta architecture.\nNote that the maximum amount of shared memory per thread block is smaller than the maximum shared memory partition available per SM. The 1 KB of shared memory not made available to a thread block is reserved for system use.\n16.8.4.\nFeatures Accelerating Specialized Computations\n\nThe NVIDIA Hopper GPU architecture includes features to accelerate matrix multiply-accumulate (MMA) computations with:\nasynchronous execution of MMA instructions\nMMA instructions acting on large matrices spanning a warp-group\ndynamic reassignment of register capacity among warp-groups to support even larger matrices, and\noperand matrices accessed directly from shared memory\nThis feature set is only available within the CUDA compilation toolchain through inline PTX.\nIt is strongly recommended that applications utilize this complex feature set through CUDA-X libraries such as cuBLAS, cuDNN, or cuFFT.\nIt is strongly recommended that device kernels utilize this complex feature set through\nCUTLASS\n, a collection of CUDA C++ template abstractions for implementing high-performance matrix-multiplication (GEMM) and related computations at all levels and scales within CUDA.\n16.9.\nCompute Capability 10.0\n\n16.9.1.\nArchitecture\n\nA Streaming Multiprocessor (SM) consists of:\n128 FP32 cores for single-precision arithmetic operations,\n64 FP64 cores for double-precision arithmetic operations,\n64 INT32 cores for integer math,\n4 mixed-precision fifth-generation Tensor Cores supporting\nFP8\ninput type in either\nE4M3\nor\nE5M2\nfor exponent (E) and mantissa (M), half-precision (fp16),\n__nv_bfloat16\n,\ntf32\n, INT8 and double precision (fp64) matrix arithmetic (see\nWarp Matrix Functions\nfor details) with sparsity support,\n16 special function units for single-precision floating-point transcendental functions,\n4 warp schedulers.\nAn SM statically distributes its warps among its schedulers. Then, at every instruction issue time, each scheduler issues one instruction for one of its assigned warps that is ready to execute, if any.\nAn SM has:\na read-only constant cache that is shared by all functional units and speeds up reads from the constant memory space, which resides in device memory,\na unified data cache and shared memory with a total size of 256 KB for devices of compute support hardware-accelerated\n\natomic accesses to CPU-resident memory. This implies that atomic accesses to host memory\n\ndo not have to be emulated with a page fault.\n\nFor these devices, the attribute\ncudaDevAttrHostNativeAtomicSupported\nis set to 1.\n19.2.2.4.\nAtomic accesses & synchronization primitives\n\nCUDA Unified Memory supports all atomic operations available to host and device threads,\n\nenabling all threads to cooperate by concurrently accessing the same shared memory location.\n\nThe\nCUDA C++ standard library\nprovides many heterogeneous synchronization primitives tuned for concurrent use between host and device threads,\n\nincluding\ncuda::atomic\n,\ncuda::atomic_ref\n,\ncuda::barrier\n,\ncuda::semaphore\n, among many others.\nOn systems without\nCPU and GPU page tables: hardware coherency vs. software coherency\n,\n\natomic accesses from the device to file-backed host memory are not supported.\n\nThe following example code is valid on systems with\nCPU and GPU page tables: hardware coherency vs. software coherency\nbut exhibits undefined behavior on other systems:\n#include\n<cuda/atomic>\n#include\n<cstdio>\n#includ", "excerpt_ranges": [[488000, 492000], [500000, 504000], [516000, 524000], [528000, 536000], [588000, 589863], [964000, 968000], [1064000, 1068000]], "range_unit": "zero-based half-open characters in extracted text", "extracted_characters": 1116212, "selected_characters": 33863, "extraction": "UTF-8 text", "selection_reason": "Ranked 4000-character windows by current entry title/tags/questions and algorithm/mechanism keywords; ties retain source order."}, {"path": "references/S-ROCM/fulltext.txt", "sha256": "f5364f72aaaf3149705e29af60d62c448b0f60b6da9e69a470090c68236af29e", "reading_scope": "full", "category": "source_original", "content": "\n===== docs/understand/hardware_implementation.rst =====\n.. meta::\n  :description: This chapter describes the hardware implementation of AMD GPUs supported by HIP.\n  :keywords: AMD, ROCm, HIP, hardware, GPU, architecture, compute unit, VALU, SALU, cache, memory hierarchy, CDNA, RDNA\n\n.. _hardware_implementation:\n\n*******************************************************************************\nHardware implementation\n*******************************************************************************\n\nThis topic describes the hardware architecture of AMD GPUs supported by HIP,\nfocusing on the internal organization and operation of GPU hardware components.\nUnderstanding these hardware details helps you optimize GPU applications and\nachieve maximum performance.\n\nOverall GPU architecture\n========================\n\nAMD GPUs consist of interconnected blocks of digital circuits that work\ntogether to execute complex parallel computing tasks. Unlike central\nprocessing units (CPUs), which dedicate significant silicon area to\ninstruction flow control, branch prediction, and complex caching hierarchies,\nGPUs allocate the majority of their die area to arithmetic pipelines. This\ndesign choice enables extreme throughput density for data-parallel workloads.\nThe architecture is organized hierarchically to enable massive parallelism\nwhile efficiently managing resources.\n\nCommand processor and control\n-----------------------------\n\nThe command processor (CP) serves as the primary interface between the CPU and\nGPU, receiving and distributing commands for execution. The CP consists of two\nmain components:\n\n* **Command processor fetcher (CPF)**: Fetches commands from memory and passes\n  them to the command processor packet processor (CPC) for processing.\n* **Command processor packet processor (CPC)**: A microcontroller that decodes\n  the fetched commands and dispatches kernels to the workgroup processors for\n  scheduling.\n\nThe command processor handles several types of operations:\n\n* Kernel launches, which are forwarded to asynchronous compute engines (ACEs)\n* Memory transfers, which are delegated to direct memory access (DMA) engines\n* Synchronization operations and memory fences\n\n**DMA engines** handle memory transfers between CPU and GPU memory without CPU\ninvolvement after initialization. Most GPUs contain two DMA engines, enabling\nconcurrent bidirectional transfers to better utilize PCIe bandwidth. The DMA\nengines fetch data in small chunks and can process transfers in parallel but\ncannot handle multiple copy commands on the same engine simultaneously.\n\n**Asynchronous compute engines (ACEs)** break down kernels into workgroups for\ndistribution to shader processor input (SPI) blocks. Multiple ACEs enable\nconcurrent kernel execution, with each ACE capable of dispatching one kernel\nat a time. ACEs process commands from different queues asynchronously,\nenabling overlap between different kernel executions and memory operations.\n\nHierarchical organization\n-------------------------\n\nThe GPU organizes compute resources in a three-level hierarchy that enables\nmodular design and resource sharing:\n\n1. **Shader engines (SE)**: Top-level organizational units containing multiple\n   shader arrays and shared resources\n2. **Shader arrays**: Groups of compute units (CUs) sharing instruction and\n   scalar caches\n3. **Compute units (CU)**: Basic execution units containing the arithmetic\n   logic units (ALUs) and registers for thread execution\n\n.. figure:: ../data/understand/hardware_implementation/selayout.png\n   :align: center\n   :alt: Diagram showing the hierarchical organization of compute units\n         grouped into shader engines on AMD GPUs\n   :width: 800\n\n   Hierarchical organization of compute units into shader engines\n\nThis hierarchical design allows different GPU configurations using the same\nunderlying architecture.\n\nShader engine components\n========================\n\nShader engines group multiple compute units together, sharing resources to improve efficiency and reduce redundancy. Each shader engine contains\nseveral key components shared across its compute units.\n\nWorkgroup manager (SPI)\n-----------------------\n\nThe workgroup manager, also called the shader processor input (SPI), bridges\nthe command processor and compute units. After the CP processes a kernel\ndispatch, the SPI:\n\n* Receives workgroups from the ACEs\n* Schedules workgroups onto available compute units\n* Initializes registers with kernel parameters\n* Ensures all warps of a workgroup execute on the same CU for synchronization\n* Monitors resource availability and queues workgroups when resources are\n  exhausted\n\nThe SPI tracks four critical resources that limit concurrent execution:\n\n* warp slots (execution contexts)\n* Vector general-purpose registers (VGPRs)\n* Scalar general-purpose registers (SGPRs)\n* Local data share (LDS) memory\n\nWorkgroup-to-CU mapping is non-deterministic and based on available resources.\nYou should not assume any specific mapping pattern, as the same kernel\nlaunched multiple times can have different workgroup distributions.\n\n.. _sl1:\n\nScalar L1 data cache (sL1D)\n---------------------------\n\nThe scalar L1 data cache (sL1D) serves scalar memory operations from multiple\nCUs within a shader array. The sL1D is shared between CUs and caches data that\nis uniform across a warp, including:\n\n* Kernel arguments and pointers\n* Grid and block dimensions\n* Constants accessed uniformly across threads\n* Data from ``__constant__`` memory when accessed uniformly\n\nUnlike the vector L1 cache, the sL1D doesn't use a \"hit-on-miss\" approach,\nmeaning subsequent requests to the same pending cache line count as duplicated\nmisses rather than hits.\n\nL1 instruction cache (L1I)\n--------------------------\n\nThe L1 instruction cache (L1I) is a read-only cache shared between multiple\nCUs in a shader array. Like the sL1D, it's backed by the L2 cache and doesn't\nuse the \"hit-on-miss\" approach. The L1I stores kernel instructions fetched by\nthe compute units, reducing instruction fetch latency and L2 cache pressure.\n\n.. _compute_unit:\n\nCompute unit architecture\n=========================\n\nThe compute unit (CU) is the fundamental execution block of AMD GPUs, serving\nas the atomic building block for massive parallelism. Each CU is responsible\nfor executing kernels through its various specialized components and\npipelines. Data flows into these pipelines, undergoes arithmetic\ntransformation, and exits as results, to maximize the number\nof such transformations per clock cycle.\n\nCUs enable latency hiding through massive hardware multithreading. A single CU\ncan manage thousands of concurrent threads organized as a number of warps, each\ncontaining 32 (RDNA) or 64 (CDNA) threads. This massive concurrency allows the\nhardware to hide memory access latency by executing other warps while some wait\nfor data.\n\n.. figure:: ../data/understand/hardware_implementation/gcn_compute_unit.png\n   :align: center\n   :alt: Detailed diagram of an AMD CDNA compute unit showing internal\n         components and data flow\n   :width: 800\n\n   Internal architecture of an AMD CDNA compute unit\n\n.. _wave-scheduling:\n\nSequencer and scheduling\n------------------------\n\nThe instruction sequencer (SQ) serves as the control center of each compute\nunit, managing instruction flow through the execution pipelines. The sequencer\nmaintains warp state and coordinates instruction execution across different\nfunctional units.\n\n**Warp organization**: The sequencer organizes active warps into four pools,\neach containing slots for up to ten warps (eight on the CDNA2 MI200 Series).\nEach slot includes:\n\n* Warp-level registers (program counter, execution mask, and others)\n* Instruction buffer for prefetched instructions\n* State information for scheduling decisions\n\nThis organization theoretically allows up to 40 concurrent warps per CU, though\nactual occupancy is typically limited by register and LDS usage.\n\n**Instruction fetching**: The fetch arbiter selects one warp per cycle to fetch\ninstructions from memory, prioritizing the oldest warps. Each CU can fetch up to\n32 bytes (4-8 instructions) per cycle.\n\n**Instruction issuing**: The issue arbiter determines which instructions\nexecute each cycle, selecting warps from one pool per cycle in round-robin\nfashion. The arbiter can issue multiple instructions per cycle to different\nexecution units, with a theoretical maximum of five instructions per cycle:\n\n* One VALU instruction\n* One vector memory operation\n* One SALU and/or scalar memory operation\n* One LDS operation\n* One branch operation\n\nInstructions always issue at warp granularity, with all threads in the warp\nexecuting the same instruction in lockstep. The hardware can perform\nsingle-cycle context switching between warps with zero overhead, as all warp\ncontexts remain resident on the CU. This enables efficient latency hiding,\nallowing the CU to switch to another warp immediately when the current warp\nencounters a stall condition such as a memory access.\n\nExecution pipelines\n-------------------\n\nEach CU contains multiple specialized execution pipelines that\nprocess different types of instructions in parallel, enabling efficient\nutilization of the hardware resources.\n\n.. _valu:\n\nVector arithmetic logic unit (VALU)\n^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^\n\nThe VALU executes vector instructions across entire warps, with each thread\npotentially operating on different data. For CDNA architectures, the VALU\nconsists of:\n\n* **Four SIMD processors**: Each containing 16 single-precision ALUs (or\n  equivalent), for 64 total ALUs per CU. In CDNA3, these are SIMD64 pipelines\n  that can execute 256 operations per cycle per CU.\n* **Vector register files**: 256-512 KiB of VGPR storage split across the\n  four SIMDs. VGPRs are organized as 32-bit lanes, providing flexibility for\n  mixed-precision computations.\n* **Instruction buffers**: Storage for up to 8-10 warps per SIMD\n\nOn architectures with 64-thread warps and 16-instruction wide SIMD units,\nexecuting one instruction takes four cycles (one cycle per 16 threads). The\nfour SIMD design ensures full utilization when sufficient warps are available,\nas a new instruction can issue to each SIMD every cycle.\n\nThe VALU serves as the primary arithmetic engine, executing the majority of\ncomputation in GPU kernels. Data flows into these pipelines, undergoes\narithmetic transformation, and exits as results, with the goal of maximizing\nthe number of such transformations per clock cycle.\n\nFor CDNA architectures with matrix operations, the VALU also dispatches matrix\nfused multiply-add (MFMA) instructions to specialized matrix units.\n\nRegister pressure and occupancy\n^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^\n\nRegister usage directly impacts CU occupancy. Each warp requires a\nportion of the finite VGPR and SGPR pools. Higher register usage per thread\nreduces the maximum number of concurrent warps, potentially limiting the CU's\nability to hide latency. Mixed-precision workloads can optimize register usage\nby storing lower-precision values in fewer registers.\n\nScalar arithmetic logic unit (SALU)\n^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^\n\nThe SALU executes instructions uniformly across all threads in a warp, handling\noperations such as:\n\n* Control flow (branches, loops)\n* Address calculations\n* Loading kernel arguments and constants\n* Managing warp-uniform values\n\nThe SALU includes:\n\n* A scalar processor for arithmetic and logic operations\n* 12.5 KiB of SGPR storage per CU\n* A scalar memory (SMEM) unit for memory operations\n\nScalar operations reduce pressure on vector units and registers by handling\nuniform computations efficiently.\n\nVector memory unit (VMEM)\n^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^\n\nThe VMEM unit handles all vector memory operations, including loads, stores,\nand atomic operations. Each thread supplies its own address and data, though\nthe hardware optimizes access through memory coalescing when threads access\nnearby addresses. The VMEM unit connects to the vector L1 cache and implements\nboth address generation and coalescing logic.\n\nBranch unit\n^^^^^^^^^^^\n\nThe branch unit executes jumps and branches for control flow changes affecting\nentire warps. Note that the branch unit handles warp-level control flow, not\nexecution mask updates for thread divergence, which are handled through\npredication.\n\n.. _sfu:\n\nSpecial function unit (SFU)\n^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^\n\nThe special function units accelerate certain arithmetic operations that are\ntoo complex and/or costly to implement purely within the standard vector ALUs.\n\nSFUs are responsible for executing transcendental and reciprocal mathematical\nfunctions, operations such as ``exp``, ``log``, ``sin``, ``cos``, ``rcp``\n(reciprocal), and ``rsqrt`` (reciprocal square root). These are heavily used\nin scientific, physics, and machine learning workloads, particularly in\nactivation functions such as GELU, sigmoid, and/or softmax.\n\nEach CU includes a set of specialized pipelines and/or transcendental\nfunction units (TFUs) that handle these operations with dedicated hardware.\nWhile their throughput is lower than that of the primary SIMD pipelines, they\nenable these functions to execute efficiently without consuming general ALU\nbandwidth.\n\nFrom the compiler's perspective, these operations map to specific AMDGPU ISA\ninstructions, such as:\n\n* ``v_exp_f32`` - compute exponential base e\n* ``v_log_f32`` - compute natural logarithm\n* ``v_sin_f32``, ``v_cos_f32`` - compute sine and/or cosine\n* ``v_rsq_f32``, ``v_rcp_f32`` - compute reciprocal and/or reciprocal square\n  root\n\nIn CDNA3-based GPUs (such as MI300), SFU throughput and latency have been\ntuned for deep learning primitives. For instance, exponentiation (``exp``) and\nlogarithm (``log``) functions are now pipelined to complete in a few cycles\nper lane, allowing vectorized activation functions in large-scale matrix\nworkloads to execute without significant stalls.\n\nFor programmers targeting ROCm and/or HIP, these SFU-accelerated operations\nare typically accessed through math intrinsics such as ``__expf``, ``__logf``,\nand/or ``__sinf``, which the compiler lowers to the corresponding AMDGPU ISA\ninstructions at compile time.\n\n.. _lsu:\n\nLoad/store unit (LSU)\n^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^\n\nThe load/store units handle the transfer of data between the compute units and\nthe GPU's memory subsystems. They are responsible for issuing, tracking, and\nretiring memory operations, including loads from and stores to global memory,\nlocal shared memory, and caches, for thousands of concurrent threads.\n\nEach CU includes a set of LSUs tightly integrated with its vector\nand scalar pipelines. These units handle memory instructions generated by\nactive warps, such as ``buffer_load``, ``buffer_store``, and\n``flat_load_dword``, and route them through the GPU's hierarchical memory\nsystem.\n\nThe LSU's responsibilities include:\n\n* Managing vector memory accesses for SIMD instructions\n* Coordinating local data share (LDS) reads and writes\n* Accessing the L0 and/or L1 caches and forwarding requests to the L2 cache\n  and high-bandwidth memory (HBM)\n* Handling synchronization and atomic operations between threads and workgroups\n\nLSUs manage thousands of outstanding memory requests per GPU, dynamically\nscheduling them to hide memory latency. While arithmetic pipelines continue\nexecuting other warps, the LSUs maintain queues of pending transactions and\nreorder responses as data returns from memory.\n\n.. _mfma_units:\n\nMatrix fused multiply-add (MFMA)\n^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^\n\nCDNA architectures (MI100 and newer) include specialized matrix acceleration\nunits for high-throughput matrix operations. These units execute independently\nfrom other VALU operations, allowing overlap between matrix and vector\ncomputations. MFMA units support various data types including ``INT8``,\n``FP16``, ``BF16``, and ``FP32``, with different throughput characteristics\nfor each.\n\nMatrix cores are GPU execution units that perform large-scale matrix\noperations in a single instruction. In AMD architectures, these units are\nformally known as MFMA (matrix fused multiply-add) units, the core hardware\nblocks responsible for accelerating deep learning, high-performance computing\n(HPC), and dense linear-algebra workloads on modern Instinct GPUs.\n\nOperating on entire tiles of matrices per instruction allows MFMA units to\ndeliver far greater arithmetic throughput and energy efficiency than scalar\nand/or vector ALUs. Rather than fetching and decoding thousands of per-element\nmultiply-add instructions, each MFMA instruction processes an entire matrix\nfragment, drastically reducing power per operation and increasing overall\nthroughput. The MFMA units implement a mini-systolic array design that\nefficiently processes matrix tiles.\n\nAn example MFMA instruction from the AMDGPU ISA is:\n\n.. code-block:: amdgpu\n\n   v_mfma_f32_16x16x4f16 v[0:15], v[16:31], v[32:47], v[0:15]\n\nThis instruction performs a matrix multiplication and accumulation\n:math:`\\pmb{D}=\\pmb{A} \\times \\pmb{B} + \\pmb{C}`, where the fragments\n:math:`\\pmb{A}`, :math:`\\pmb{B}`, and :math:`\\pmb{C}` are stored in VGPRs.\nThe suffix ``16x16x4f16`` indicates a tile size of :math:`16 \\times 16`, with\nan inner dimension of :math:`4`, operating on half-precision (FP16) inputs and\naccumulating into 32-bit floating-point outputs.\n\nProgrammers can access MFMA functionality at multiple levels: through\noptimized libraries, compiler intrinsics, and/or inline assembly, providing\nflexibility for different use cases.\n\nThe MFMA units use both standard VGPRs and additional accumulation VGPRs\n(AGPRs) on supported architectures, providing up to 512 KiB of combined\nregister storage per CU.\n\n.. _dme:\n\nData movement engine (CDNA 3 / CDNA 4)\n^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^\n\nCDNA 3 and CDNA 4 architectures include specialized Data Movement Engine (DME)\nhardware units designed to accelerate access to multi-dimensional tensor data\nin GPU memory. DMEs perform high-throughput, low-overhead copies between\nglobal memory (HBM) and the on-chip memory hierarchy, particularly the Local\nData Share (LDS) and L0 and/or L1 caches, without consuming compute resources.\n\nA DME issues bulk memory transactions for contiguous and/or affine data\nregions, such as tensors laid out as multi-dimensional arrays in global\nmemory. The hardware computes memory addresses for large block transfers in\nparallel, offloading this work from the SIMD pipelines and reducing pressure\non both the register file and the instruction scheduler. This enables higher\nsustained bandwidth and lower latency for operations involving tiled matrix\nand/or tensor data.\n\nIn practice, DMEs handle transfers of the form\n:math:`\\text{addr}=\\text{stride} \\times \\text{index} + \\text{offset}` across\nmany threads and dimensions simultaneously. By performing these affine address\ncalculations directly in hardware, the DME avoids the need for per-thread\naddress arithmetic, freeing up scalar ALUs and registers for computation.\n\nThe DME design provides two key advantages:\n\n* **Resource decoupling**: By removing large tensor copies from the main\n  execution pipelines, the CU can continue executing arithmetic instructions\n  while data movement occurs in the background.\n* **Asynchronous execution model**: A single warp can issue a DME copy command,\n  immediately resume computation, and later synchronize only when the transfer\n  has completed. This enables producer-consumer parallelism.\n\nProgrammers can access this functionality through asynchronous copy intrinsics\nin ROCm, such as ``__builtin_amdgcn_async_work_group_copy``, which map\ndirectly to hardware-level DME operations. These intrinsics allow explicit\ncontrol over data transfer overlap, synchronization, and cache placement.\n\n.. _lds:\n\nLocal data share (LDS)\n----------------------\n\nThe local data share provides fast on-CU scratchpad memory for communication\nbetween threads in a workgroup.\n\n.. figure:: ../data/understand/hardware_implementation/lds.svg\n   :align: center\n   :alt: Diagram showing the organization of local data share with banks and\n         connections to SIMD units\n   :width: 800\n\n   Local data share organization and SIMD connections\n\n**Organization**: The LDS contains 32 (CDNA, CDNA 2, and CDNA 3) or 64 (CDNA 4\nand RDNA 2, RDNA 3, and RDNA 4) banks, each 4-bytes wide, providing 128 (CDNA,\nCDNA 2, and CDNA 3) or 256 (CDNA 4 and RDNA 2, RDNA 3, and RDNA 4) bytes per\ncycle total bandwidth. Each bank can be accessed independently every cycle for\nreads, writes, and/or atomic operations. The SIMDs connect to the LDS in pairs,\nwith each pair sharing a 64-byte bidirectional port.\n\n**Access patterns**: A single warp can achieve up to 64 bytes per cycle\nthroughput (16 lanes per cycle). The actual bandwidth depends on data size and\naccess patterns:\n\n* 4-byte values: 8 cycles for 64 threads (50% peak bandwidth)\n* 16-byte values: 20 cycles for 64 threads (80% peak bandwidth)\n\n**Conflict resolution**: The LDS includes hardware to detect and resolve bank\nconflicts when multiple threads access different addresses in the same bank.\nConflicts are resolved by serializing accesses across multiple cycles. Address\nconflicts (multiple threads atomically updating the same address) are similarly\nserialized. Broadcasting from the same address to multiple threads is handled\nefficiently without conflicts.\n\n.. _vl1:\n\nVector L1 cache\n---------------\n\nEach CU contains a dedicated vector L1 data cache (vL1D) serving vector memory\noperations. Key characteristics include:\n\n* Write-through design (writes go directly to L2)\n* Optimization for high-bandwidth streaming access patterns\n* Coherent with other CUs through software management\n* Typical size of 16 KB per CU\n\nThe vector cache tags are checked for all vector memory operations, with\nmisses forwarded to the L2 cache. The write-through design simplifies\ncoherence at the cost of write bandwidth.\n\nMemory hierarchy and system\n===========================\n\nThe GPU memory system provides the bandwidth and capacity needed for massive\nparallel computation while managing data coherence and access efficiency.\n\n.. _hbm:\n\nMemory organization\n-------------------\n\n.. figure:: ../data/understand/hardware_implementation/cdna2_gcd.png\n  :alt: Block diagram showing four compute engines with L2 cache, memory\n        controllers, and Infinity Fabric interconnect on CDNA2\n  :width: 800\n\n  CDNA2 Graphics Compute Die organization showing memory subsystem\n\nAMD GPUs typically use high-bandwidth memory (HBM) for data-intensive\nworkloads, providing significantly higher bandwidth than traditional GDDR\nmemory at the cost of slightly higher latency. HBM achieves this through\nvertical stacking of memory dies and wide memory buses, enabling massive\nparallel access to memory channels.\n\nThe memory system includes:\n\n* **Memory channels**: Multiple independent memory controllers (typically 8-16)\n* **L2 cache banks**: Distributed cache banks serving as the coherence point\n* **Infinity Fabric**: High-speed interconnect for data routing\n\nL2 cache architecture\n---------------------\n\nThe L2 cache serves as the coherence point for all GPU memory accesses and is\nshared by all compute units. The L2 consists of multiple independent channels\n(32 on CDNA GPUs at 256-byte interleaving) that operate in parallel.\n\n.. figure:: ../data/understand/hardware_implementation/l2perf_model.png\n   :align: center\n   :alt: Diagram showing L2 cache to Infinity Fabric transaction flow with\n         request categorization and routing\n   :width: 800\n\n   L2 cache to Infinity Fabric transaction flow\n\n**Key characteristics**:\n\n* **Channel organization**: Each channel handles a portion of the address\n  space, with addresses interleaved across channels for load balancing.\n* **Hit-on-miss behavior**: If a request arrives for a pending cache line\n  fill, it counts as a hit, improving the effective hit rate.\n* **Write coalescing**: Multiple writes to the same cache line are combined.\n* **Atomic operation support**: Atomics execute directly in the L2 cache for\n  coherence.\n\n**L2-Fabric interface**: Requests missing in L2 are routed through Infinity\nFabric to the appropriate memory location, which could be:\n\n* Local HBM on the same GPU\n* Remote GPU memory (in multi-GPU systems)\n* System memory (CPU DRAM)\n\nThe interface categorizes requests by type (read and/or write), size (32B\nand/or 64B), and destination for optimal routing.\n\n.. _memory_coherence:\n\nMemory coherence\n----------------\n\nGPU memory coherence differs significantly from CPU designs to optimize for\nthroughput over latency:\n\n**Write-through L1 caches**: All writes update both L1 and L2, ensuring L2\nalways has the latest data. This eliminates the need for complex coherence\nprotocols between L1 caches but requires higher write bandwidth.\n\n**Software-managed coherence**: Coherence between CUs requires explicit\nsynchronization through:\n\n* Memory fences for ordering\n* Cache invalidation instructions\n* Atomic operations (executed at L2 level)\n* Kernel boundaries (implicit synchronization)\n\n**Write combining**: To handle partial cache line updates from different CUs,\nthe GPU uses write masks indicating which bytes to update. This prevents false\nsharing issues while maintaining correctness.\n\nMemory coalescing\n-----------------\n\nMemory coalescing combines memory accesses from multiple threads into fewer\ntransactions, significantly improving bandwidth utilization. The coalescing\nhardware in the VMEM unit analyzes addresses from all threads in a warp and\ngroups them into the minimum number of cache line requests.\n\n**Coalesced access pattern**: When consecutive threads access consecutive\nmemory addresses, the hardware can combine all 64 thread requests into as few\nas 4-8 cache line requests (depending on data size and alignment).\n\n**Non-coalesced access pattern**: When threads access widely separated\naddresses, each thread can generate a separate memory transaction, reducing\neffective bandwidth by up to 16x or more.\n\nTo achieve optimal memory performance:\n\n* Ensure consecutive threads access consecutive memory addresses\n* Align data structures to cache line boundaries (64B and/or 128B)\n* Use structure-of-arrays rather than array-of-structures layouts\n* Consider padding to avoid bank conflicts\n\nArchitecture variants\n=====================\n\nAMD supports multiple GPU architecture families optimized for different use\ncases while maintaining HIP compatibility.\n\n.. _cdna_architecture:\n\nCDNA architecture\n-----------------\n\nCDNA (Compute DNA) specializes in high-performance computing and machine\nlearning workloads. Key features include:\n\n.. figure:: ../data/understand/hardware_implementation/cdna3_cu.png\n  :alt: Block diagram showing CDNA3 compute unit with matrix core unit, shader\n        cores, L1 cache, and local data share\n  :width: 800\n\n  CDNA3 compute unit with matrix acceleration\n\n**Matrix Core Unit**: Specialized hardware for matrix multiply-accumulate\noperations, providing significantly more throughput than vector units for\nsupported operations. Matrix cores support multiple precisions (``INT8``,\n``FP16``, ``BF16``, ``FP32``) with varying performance characteristics.\n\n**Accumulation VGPRs (AGPRs)**: Additional register file space (up to 256 KB)\ndedicated to matrix accumulation, doubling the available register storage for\nmatrix operations. Data movement between VGPRs and AGPRs uses specialized\ninstructions (``v_accvgpr_*``).\n\n**Enhanced memory bandwidth**: CDNA GPUs typically use HBM2, HBM2e, and/or\nHBM3 memory technology.\n\n**Multi-die designs**: CDNA2 (MI250) and CDNA3 (MI300) use chiplet\narchitectures with multiple dies connected through high-speed links, scaling\nto higher compute and memory capacities.\n\n.. _rdna_architecture:\n\nRDNA architecture\n-----------------\n\nRDNA optimizes for graphics and lower-latency compute workloads through\nfundamental architectural changes:\n\n.. figure:: ../data/understand/hardware_implementation/rdna3_cu.png\n  :alt: Block diagram showing RDNA3 work group processor with dual compute\n        units, shared caches, and 32-wide SIMD units\n  :width: 800\n\n  RDNA3 work group processor architecture\n\n**Wave32 execution**: Primary execution mode uses 32-thread warps, reducing\ndivergence penalties and register pressure.\n\n**Dual compute units**: The work group processor (WGP) replaces standalone\nCUs, containing two closely coupled compute units sharing resources:\n\n* Each CU has two 32-wide SIMD units\n* Warps execute in a single cycle on 32-wide SIMDs\n* Reduced instruction latency improves responsiveness\n\n**Three-level cache hierarchy**:\n\n* **L0 cache**: Per-CU cache\n* **L1 cache**: Shared between CUs in a WGP (new intermediate level)\n* **L2 cache**: Global cache shared across all WGPs\n\n**128-byte cache lines**: Aligning with Wave32 access patterns (32 threads × 4\nbytes = 128 bytes).\n\nThese RDNA optimizations target gaming workloads where latency matters more\nthan pure throughput, though the architecture remains capable for general\ncompute tasks.\n\nPerformance considerations\n==========================\n\nUnderstanding hardware characteristics helps you optimize GPU applications for\nmaximum performance.\n\nOccupancy and resource limits\n-----------------------------\n\nOccupancy measures the ratio of active warps to maximum possible\nwarps on a CU. Higher occupancy generally improves latency hiding but is\nlimited by:\n\n* **Register usage**: Each warp requires VGPRs and SGPRs from finite\n  pools\n* **LDS allocation**: Shared memory used per workgroup\n* **warp slots**: Fixed number of execution contexts per CU\n* **Workgroup size**: Smaller workgroups can waste resources\n\nBalancing these resources is critical for achieving optimal occupancy. Tools\nsuch as ``rocprofv3`` can help analyze occupancy and identify limiting\nfactors.\n\nLatency hiding through multithreading\n-------------------------------------\n\nGPUs hide memory and instruction latency through massive hardware multithreading\nrather than complex CPU techniques such as out-of-order execution and/or\nspeculation. With sufficient warps:\n\n* Memory latency is hidden by executing other warps during waits\n* Pipeline latencies are covered by round-robin warp scheduling\n* No context switch overhead as all contexts remain resident\n\nThe hardware can switch between warps every cycle, maintaining high ALU\nutilization even with long-latency operations in flight.\n\nMemory bandwidth utilization\n----------------------------\n\nEffective memory bandwidth depends on access patterns:\n\n* **Coalesced access**: Can achieve 70-90% of peak bandwidth\n* **Random access**: Might achieve only 5-15% of peak bandwidth\n* **Bank conflicts**: Can serialize LDS access, reducing throughput\n\nMemory-bound kernels should focus on:\n\n* Maximizing coalescing through proper data layout\n* Prefetching and data reuse in LDS\n* Balancing computation with memory access\n* Using appropriate cache policies\n\nHardware-specific optimizations\n-------------------------------\n\nDifferent AMD GPU architectures benefit from tailored optimizations:\n\n**For CDNA**:\n\n* Optimize for 64-thread warp granularity\n* Leverage matrix cores for applicable algorithms\n* Consider AGPR usage for register spilling\n\n**For RDNA**:\n\n* Design for 32-thread warp execution\n* Utilize improved divergence handling\n* Take advantage of additional cache level\n\n**Architecture-agnostic**:\n\n* Minimize divergent control flow\n* Ensure memory access coalescing\n* Balance resource usage for occupancy\n* Overlap computation with memory access\n\nSummary\n=======\n\nAMD GPU hardware architecture provides massive parallelism through\nhierarchical organization of compute resources, specialized execution units,\nand a sophisticated memory system. Understanding these hardware details, from\nthe command processor through shader engines to individual compute units and\nthe memory hierarchy, enables you to write more efficient GPU applications.\n\nKey hardware concepts for optimization include:\n\n* Workgroup scheduling and resource management by the SPI\n* Instruction scheduling and warp execution in compute units\n* Memory coalescing and cache behavior\n* Architecture-specific features (matrix cores, Wave32 and/or Wave64 modes)\n* Resource limits affecting occupancy\n\nFor details on mapping parallel algorithms to this hardware, see the\n:ref:`programming_model` chapter. For specific optimization techniques,\nconsult the performance optimization guides in the ROCm documentation.\n", "excerpt_ranges": [[0, 32330]], "range_unit": "zero-based half-open characters in extracted text", "extracted_characters": 32330, "selected_characters": 32330, "extraction": "UTF-8 text", "selection_reason": "full extracted text fits allocation"}, {"path": "references/S-TORCH-NUMERIC/fulltext.txt", "sha256": "bb6b2d8154a10f8f3b15e5ae4465cbc8d833067046964fc6b4d66dc036131b45", "reading_scope": "full", "category": "source_original", "content": "\n===== docs/source/notes/numerical_accuracy.rst =====\n.. _numerical_accuracy:\n\nNumerical accuracy\n==================\n\nIn modern computers, floating point numbers are represented using IEEE 754 standard.\nFor more details on floating point arithmetic and IEEE 754 standard, please see\n`Floating point arithmetic <https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Floating-point_arithmetic>`_\nIn particular, note that floating point provides limited accuracy (about 7 decimal digits\nfor single precision floating point numbers, about 16 decimal digits for double precision\nfloating point numbers) and that floating point addition and multiplication are not\nassociative, so the order of the operations affects the results.\nBecause of this, PyTorch is not guaranteed\nto produce bitwise identical results for floating point computations that are\nmathematically identical. Similarly, bitwise identical results are not guaranteed across\nPyTorch releases, individual commits, or different platforms. In particular, CPU and GPU\nresults can be different even for bitwise-identical inputs and even after controlling for\nthe sources of randomness.\n\nBatched computations or slice computations\n------------------------------------------\n\nMany operations in PyTorch support batched computation, where the same operation is performed\nfor the elements of the batches of inputs. An example of this is :meth:`torch.mm` and\n:meth:`torch.bmm`. It is possible to implement batched computation as a loop over batch elements,\nand apply the necessary math operations to the individual batch elements, for efficiency reasons\nwe are not doing that, and typically perform computation for the whole batch. The mathematical\nlibraries that we are calling, and PyTorch internal implementations of operations can produce\nslightly different results in this case, compared to non-batched computations. In particular,\nlet ``A`` and ``B`` be 3D tensors with the dimensions suitable for batched matrix multiplication.\nThen ``(A@B)[0]`` (the first element of the batched result) is not guaranteed to be bitwise\nidentical to ``A[0]@B[0]`` (the matrix product of the first elements of the input batches)\neven though mathematically it's an identical computation.\n\nSimilarly, an operation applied to a tensor slice is not guaranteed to produce results that are\nidentical to the slice of the result of the same operation applied to the full tensor. E.g. let\n``A`` be a 2-dimensional tensor. ``A.sum(-1)[0]`` is not guaranteed to be bitwise equal to\n``A[:,0].sum()``.\n\n\nExtremal values\n---------------\n\nWhen inputs contain large values such that intermediate results may overflow the range of the\nused datatype, the end result may overflow too, even though it is representable in the original\ndatatype. E.g.:\n\n.. code:: python\n\n    import torch\n    a=torch.tensor([1e20, 1e20]) # fp32 type by default\n    a.norm() # produces tensor(inf)\n    a.double().norm() # produces tensor(1.4142e+20, dtype=torch.float64), representable in fp32\n\n.. _Linear Algebra Stability:\n\nLinear algebra (``torch.linalg``)\n---------------------------------\n\nNon-finite values\n\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\n\nThe external libraries (backends) that ``torch.linalg`` uses provide no guarantees on their behaviour\nwhen the inputs have non-finite values like ``inf`` or ``NaN``. As such, neither does PyTorch.\nThe operations may return a tensor with non-finite values, or raise an exception, or even segfault.\n\nConsider using :func:`torch.isfinite` before calling these functions to detect this situation.\n\nExtremal values in linalg\n\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\"\n\nFunctions within ``torch.linalg`` have more `Extremal Values`_ than other PyTorch functions.\n\n:ref:`linalg solvers` and :ref:`linalg inverses` assume that the input matrix ``A`` is invertible. If it is close to\nbeing non-invertible (for example, if it has a very small singular value), then these algorithms may silently return\nincorrect results. These matrices are said to be `ill-conditioned <https://nhigham.com/2020/03/19/what-is-a-condition-number/>`_.\nIf provided with ill-conditioned inputs, the result of these functions they may vary when using the same inputs on different\ndevices or when using different backends via the keyword ``driver``.\n\nSpectral operations like ``svd``, ``eig``, and ``eigh`` may also return incorrect results (and their gradients may be infinite)\nwhen their inputs have singular values that are close to each other. This is because the algorithms used to compute these decompositions\nstruggle to converge for these inputs.\n\nRunning the computation in ``float64`` (as NumPy does by default) often helps, but it does not solve these issues in all cases.\nAnalyzing the spectrum of the inputs via :func:`torch.linalg.svdvals` or their condition number via :func:`torch.linalg.cond`\nmay help to detect these issues.\n\n\nTensorFloat-32(TF32) on Nvidia Ampere (and later) devices\n---------------------------------------------------------\n\nOn Ampere (and later) Nvidia GPUs, PyTorch can use TensorFloat32 (TF32) to speed up mathematically intensive operations, in particular matrix multiplications and convolutions.\nWhen an operation is performed using TF32 tensor cores, only the first 10 bits of the input mantissa are read.\nThis may reduce accuracy and produce surprising results (e.g., multiplying a matrix by the identity matrix may produce results that are different from the input).\nBy default, TF32 tensor cores are disabled for matrix multiplications and enabled for convolutions, although most neural network workloads have the same convergence behavior when using TF32 as they have with fp32.\nWe recommend enabling TF32 tensor cores for matrix multiplications with ``torch.backends.cuda.matmul.fp32_precision = \"tf32\"`` (```torch.backends.cuda.matmul.allow_tf32 = True`` is going to be deprecated) if your network does not need full float32 precision.\nIf your network needs full float32 precision for both matrix multiplications and convolutions, then TF32 tensor cores can also be disabled for convolutions with ``torch.backends.cudnn.conv.fp32_precision = \"ieee\"`` (``torch.backends.cudnn.allow_tf32 = False`` is going to be deprecated).\n\nFor more information see :ref:`TensorFloat32<tf32_on_ampere>`.\n\nReduced Precision Reduction for FP16 and BF16 GEMMs\n----------------------------------------------------\nHalf-precision GEMM operations are typically done with intermediate accumulations (reduction) in single-precision for numerical accuracy and improved resilience to overflow. For performance, certain GPU architectures, especially more recent ones, allow a few truncations of the intermediate accumulation results to the reduced precision (e.g., half-precision). This change is often benign from the perspective of model convergence, though it may lead to unexpected results (e.g., ``inf`` values when the final result should be be representable in half-precision).\nIf reduced-precision reductions are problematic, they can be turned off with\n``torch.backends.cuda.matmul.allow_fp16_reduced_precision_reduction = False``\n\nA similar flag exists for BF16 GEMM operations and is turned on by default. If BF16\nreduced-precision reductions are problematic, they can be turned off with\n``torch.backends.cuda.matmul.allow_bf16_reduced_precision_reduction = False``\n\nFor more information see :ref:`allow_fp16_reduced_precision_reduction<fp16reducedprecision>` and :ref:`allow_bf16_reduced_precision_reduction<bf16reducedprecision>`\n\nReduced Precision Reduction for FP16 and BF16 in Scaled Dot Product Attention (SDPA)\n------------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nA naive SDPA math backend, when using FP16/BF16 inputs, can accumulate significant numerical errors due to the usage of low-precision intermediate buffers. To mitigate this issue, the default behavior now involves upcasting FP16/BF16 inputs to FP32. Computations are performed in FP32/TF32, and the final FP32 results are then downcasted back to FP16/BF16. This will improve numerical accuracy of the final output for the math backend with FP16/BF16 inputs, but increases memory usages and may cause the performance regressions in the math backend as computations shift from FP16/BF16 BMM to FP32/TF32 BMM/Matmul.\n\nFor scenarios where reduced-precision reductions are preferred for speed, they can be enabled with the following setting:\n``torch.backends.cuda.allow_fp16_bf16_reduction_math_sdp(True)``\n\n.. _fp16_on_mi200:\n\nReduced Precision FP16 and BF16 GEMMs and Convolutions on AMD Instinct MI200 devices\n------------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nOn AMD Instinct MI200 GPUs, the FP16 and BF16 V_DOT2 and MFMA matrix instructions flush input and output denormal values to zero. FP32 and FP64 MFMA matrix instructions do not flush input and output denormal values to zero. The affected instructions are only used by rocBLAS (GEMM) and MIOpen (convolution) kernels; all other PyTorch operations will not encounter this behavior. All other supported AMD GPUs will not encounter this behavior.\n\nrocBLAS and MIOpen provide alternate implementations for affected FP16 operations. Alternate implementations for BF16 operations are not provided; BF16 numbers have a larger dynamic range than FP16 numbers and are less likely to encounter denormal values. For the FP16 alternate implementations, FP16 input values are cast to an intermediate BF16 value and then cast back to FP16 output after the accumulate FP32 operations. In this way, the input and output types are unchanged.\n\nWhen training using FP16 precision, some models may fail to converge with FP16 denorms flushed to zero. Denormal values more frequently occur in the backward pass of training during gradient calculation. PyTorch by default will use the rocBLAS and MIOpen alternate implementations during the backward pass. The default behavior can be overridden using environment variables, ROCBLAS_INTERNAL_FP16_ALT_IMPL and MIOPEN_DEBUG_CONVOLUTION_ATTRIB_FP16_ALT_IMPL. The behavior of these environment variables is as follows:\n\n+---------------+-----------+-----------+\n|               | forward   | backward  |\n+===============+===========+===========+\n| Env unset     | original  | alternate |\n+---------------+-----------+-----------+\n| Env set to 1  | alternate | alternate |\n+---------------+-----------+-----------+\n| Env set to 0  | original  | original  |\n+---------------+-----------+-----------+\n\nThe following is the list of operations where rocBLAS may be used:\n\n* torch.addbmm\n* torch.addmm\n* torch.baddbmm\n* torch.bmm\n* torch.mm\n* torch.nn.GRUCell\n* torch.nn.LSTMCell\n* torch.nn.Linear\n* torch.sparse.addmm\n* the following torch._C._ConvBackend implementations:\n\n  * slowNd\n  * slowNd_transposed\n  * slowNd_dilated\n  * slowNd_dilated_transposed\n\nThe following is the list of operations where MIOpen may be used:\n\n* torch.nn.Conv[Transpose]Nd\n* the following torch._C._ConvBackend implementations:\n\n  * ConvBackend::Miopen\n  * ConvBackend::MiopenDepthwise\n  * ConvBackend::MiopenTranspose\n", "excerpt_ranges": [[0, 10998]], "range_unit": "zero-based half-open characters in extracted text", "extracted_characters": 10998, "selected_characters": 10998, "extraction": "UTF-8 text", "selection_reason": "full extracted text fits allocation"}, {"path": "references/S-CUDA-CAPABILITY/fulltext.txt", "sha256": "1f45872f110694d59f7e9b55ea37150648376f92ba85b0c3922b712a73736a32", "reading_scope": "excerpt", "category": "source_original", "content": "[EXCERPT extracted-character range 488000:492000]\nerations sequenced before this call have completed, the arrival count is decremented by one and hence the net effect on the arrival count is zero. It is user’s responsibility to make sure that the increment on the arrival count does not exceed\n__mbarrier_maximum_count()\n.\n7.29.\nAsynchronous Data Copies using the Tensor Memory Accelerator (TMA)\n\nMany applications require movement of large amounts of data from and to global\n\nmemory. Often, the data is laid out in global memory as a multi-dimensional\n\narray with non-sequential data acess patterns. To reduce global memory usage,\n\nsub-tiles of such arrays are copied to shared memory before use in computations.\n\nThe loading and storing involves address-calculations that can be error-prone\n\nand repetitive. To offload these computations, Compute Capability 9.0 introduces the\n\nTensor Memory Accelerator (TMA). The primary goal of TMA is to provide an efficient\n\ndata transfer mechanism from global memory to shared memory for\n\nmulti-dimensional arrays.\nNaming\n. Tensor memory accelerator (TMA) is a broad term used to refer to the\n\nfeatures described in this section. For the purpose of forward-compatibility and\n\nto reduce discrepancies with the PTX ISA, the text in this section refers to TMA\n\noperations as either bulk-asynchronous copies or bulk tensor asynchronous\n\ncopies, depending on the specific type of copy used. The term “bulk” is used to\n\ncontrast these operations with the asynchronous memory operations described in\n\nthe previous sections.\nDimensions\n. TMA supports copying both one-dimensional and multi-dimensional\n\narrays (up to 5-dimensional). The programming model for\nbulk-asynchronous\n\ncopies\nof one-dimensional contiguous arrays is different from the programming\n\nmodel for\nbulk tensor asynchronous copies\nof multi-dimensional arrays. To\n\nperform a bulk tensor asynchronous copy of a multi-dimensional array, the\n\nhardware requires a\ntensor map\n.\n\nThis object describes the layout of the multi-dimensional array in global and\n\nshared memory. A tensor map is typically created on the host using the\ncuTensorMapEncode\n\nAPI\nand then transferred from host to device as a\nconst\nkernel parameter annotated\n\nwith\n__grid_constant__\n.\n\nThe tensor map is transferred from host to device as a\nconst\nkernel\n\nparameter annotated with\n__grid_constant__\n, and can be used on the device to\n\ncopy a tile of data between shared and global memory. In contrast, performing a\n\nbulk-asynchronous copy of a contiguous one-dimensional array does not require a\n\ntensor map: it can be performed on-device with a pointer and size parameter.\nSource and destination\n. The source and destination addresses of bulk-asynchronous copy\n\noperations can be in shared or global memory. The operations can read data from global to\n\nshared memory, write data from shared to global memory, and also copy from\n\nshared memory to\nDistributed Shared Memory\nof another block in the same cluster.\n\nIn addition, when in a cluster, a bulk-asynchronous operation can be specified as being\n\nmulticast. In this case, data can be transferred from global memory to the\n\nshared memory of multiple blocks within the cluster. The multicast feature is\n\noptimized for target architecture\nsm_90a\nand may have\nsignificantly reduced performance\non\n\nother targets. Hence, it is advised to be used with\ncompute architecture\nsm_90a\n.\nAsynchronous\n. Data transfers using TMA are\nasynchronous\n. This allows the initiating\n\nthread to continue computing while the hardware asynchronously copies the data.\nWhether the data transfer occurs asynchronously in practice is up to the hardware implementation and may change in the future\n.\n\nThere are several\ncompletion mechanisms\nthat bulk-asynchronous operations can use to signal that they have completed.\n\nWhen the operation reads from global to shared memory, any\n\nthread in the block can wait for the data to be readable in shared memory by\n\nwaiting on a\nShared Memory Barrier\n. When the bulk-asynchronous\n\noperation writes data from shared m\n\n[EXCERPT extracted-character range 500000:504000]\n/ Size\nAlignment\nGlobal memory address\nMust be 16 byte aligned.\nShared memory address\nMust be 16 byte aligned.\nShared memory barrier address\nMust be 8 byte aligned (this is guaranteed by\ncuda::barrier\n).\nSize of transfer\nMust be a multiple of 16 bytes.\n7.29.2.\nUsing TMA to transfer multi-dimensional arrays\n\nThe primary difference between the one-dimensional and multi-dimensional case is\n\nthat a tensor map must be created on the host and passed to the CUDA kernel.\n\nThis section describes how to create a tensor map using the CUDA driver API, how\n\nto pass it to device, and how to use it on device.\nDriver API\n. A tensor map is created using the\ncuTensorMapEncodeTiled\ndriver API. This API can be accessed by linking to the driver directly\n\n(\n-lcuda\n) or by using the\ncudaGetDriverEntryPoint\nAPI. This layout matches the 128 bytes row length and\n\nchanges the shared memory layout in a way that both the column wise and row wise access don’t require the same banks per transaction.\nThe two tables,\nFigure 26\nand\nFigure 27\n,  below show the normal and the swizzled shared memory layout of the 8x8 matrix of type\nint4\nand\n\nits transpose matrix. The entries show the shared memory\n\nindices of the 16-byte matrix elements.\nFigure 26\nIn the shared memory data layout without swizzle, the shared memory indices are equivalent to the global memory indices.\n\nPer load instruction, one row is read and stored in a column of the transpose buffer. Since all matrix elements of the\n\ncolumn in the transpose fall in the same bank, the store must be serialized, resulting in eight store transactions, giving\n\nan eight-way bank conflict per stored column.\n\nFigure 27\nThe shared memory data layout with\nCU_TENSOR_MAP_SWIZZLE_128B\nswizzle. example is supposed to show the use of swizzle and ‘as-is’ is not performant nor does it scale beyond the given dimensions.\nExplanation.\nDuring data transfer, the TMA engine shuffles the data according to the swizzle pattern, as described in the following\n\ntables. These swizzle patterns define the mapping of the 16-byte chunks along the swizzle width to subgroups of four banks.\n\nIt is of type\nCUtensorMapSwizzle\nand has four options: none, 32 bytes, 64 bytes and 128 bytes. Note that the shared memory box’s\n\ninner dimension must be less or equal to the span of the swizzle pattern.\n7.29.3.2.\nThe Swizzle Modes\n\nAs previously mentioned, there are four swizzle modes. The following tables show the different swizzle patterns, including the relation of the new\n\nshared memory indices. The tables define the mapping of the 16-byte chunks along the 128 bytes to eight subgroups of four banks.\nFigure 28\nAn Overview of TMA Swizzle Patterns\n\nConsiderations.\nWhen applying a TMA swizzle pattern, it is crucial to adhere to specific memory requirements:\nGlobal memory alignment:\nGlobal memory must be aligned to 128 bytes.\nShared memory alignment:\nFor simplicity shared memory should be aligned according to the number of bytes after which the swizzle pattern repeats. When the shared memory buffer is not aligned by the number of bytes by which the swizzle pattern repeats itself, there is an offset between the swizzle pattern and the shared memory.\n\nSee\ncomment\n, below.\nInner dimension:\nThe inner dimension of the shared memory block must meet the size requirements specified in table\nTable 10\n. If these\n\nrequirements are not met, the instruction is considered invalid. Additionally, if the swizzle width exceeds the inner dimension,\n\nensure that the shared memory is allocated to accommodate the full swizzle width.\nGranularity:\nThe granularity of swizzle mapping is fixed at 16 bytes. This means that data is organized and accessed in chunks\n\nof 16 bytes, which must be considered when planning memory layout and access patterns.\nSwizzle Pattern Pointer Offset Computation\n. Here, we describe how to determine the offset between the swizzle pattern and the shared memory, when the shared memory buffer is not aligned by the number of bytes by which the swizzle pattern repeats itself.\n\nWhen using TMA, the shared memory is required to be aligned to 128 bytes. To find how many times the shared memory buffer relative to the swizzle pattern is shifted by that, apply the corresponding offset formula.\nTable 9\nSwizzle Pattern Pointer Offset Formula and Index Relation\n\nSwizzle Mode\nOffset Formula\nIndex Relation\nCU_TENSOR_MAP_SWIZZLE_128B\n(reinterpret_cast\n<uintptr_t>(smem_ptr)/128)%8\nsmem[y][x]\n<->\nsmem[y][((y+offset)%8)^x]\nCU_TENSOR_MAP_SWIZZLE_64B\n(reinterpret_cast\n<uintptr_t>(smem_ptr)/128)%4\nsmem[y][x]\n<->\nsmem[y][((y+offset)%4)^x]\nCU_TENSOR_MAP_SWIZZLE_32B\n(reinterpret_cast\n<uintptr_t>(smem_ptr)/128)%2\nsmem[y][x]\n<->\nsmem[y][((y+offset)%2)^x]\nIn\nFigure 28\n, this offset represents the initial row offset, thus, in the swizzle index calculation, it is added to the row index\ny\n.\n\nThe following snippet shows how to acces the swizzled s\n\n[EXCERPT extracted-character range 528000:536000]\nlace\nfunctions. These functions wrap the\ntensormap.replace\nPTX instruction, which can be used to modify any field of a tiled-type tensor map, including the\n\nbase address, size, stride, and so on.\nUsing the\ncuda::ptx::tensormap_copy_fenceproxy\nfunction, copy the modified tensor map from shared memory to global memory and perform any necessary fencing.\nThe following code contains a kernel that follows these steps. For completeness, it modifies all the fields\n\nof the tensor map. Typically, a kernel will modify just a few fields.\nIn this kernel,\ntemplate_tensor_map\nis passed as a kernel parameter. This is the preferred way of moving\ntemplate_tensor_map\nfrom the host to the device. If the kernel is intended to update an existing tensor map in device memory, it can take a\n\npointer to the existing tensor map to modify.\nNote\nThe format of the tensor map may change over time. Therefore, the\ncuda::ptx::tensormap_replace\nfunctions and corresponding\ntensormap.replace.tile\nPTX instructions are marked as specific to sm_90a. To use them, compile using\nnvcc\n-arch\nsm_90a\n....\n.\nTip\nOn sm_90a, a zero-initialized buffer in shared memory may also be used as the initial tensor map value. This\n\nenables encoding a tensor map purely on device, without using the driver API to encode the\ntemplate_tensor_map\nvalue\n.\nNote\nOn-device modification is only supported for tiled-type tensor maps; other tensor map types cannot be modified on device. of a Modified Tensor Map\n\nIn contrast to using a tensor map that is passed as a\nconst\n__grid_constant__\nkernel parameter, using a tensor map in\n\nglobal memory requires explicitly establishing a release-acquire pattern in the tensor map proxy between the threads\n\nthat modify the tensor map and the threads that use it.\nThe release part of the pattern was shown in the previous section. It is accomplished using\n\nthe\ncuda::ptx::tensormap.cp_fenceproxy\nfunction.\nThe acquire part is accomplished using the\ncuda::ptx::fence_proxy_tensormap_generic\nfunction that wraps the\nfence.proxy.tensormap::generic.acquire\ninstruction. If the two threads participating in the release-acquire pattern are on the same device, the\n.gpu\nscope suffices. If the threads are on\n\ndifferent devices, the\n.sys\nscope must be used. Once a tensor map has been acquired by one thread, it can be used by other threads in the block\n\nafter sufficient synchronization, for example, using\n__syncthreads()\n. The thread that uses the tensor map and the thread that performs the fence\n\nmust be in the same block. That is, if the threads are in, for example, two different thread blocks of the same cluster, the same grid, or a\n\ndifferent kernel, synchronization APIs such as\ncooperative_groups::cluster\nor\ngrid_group::sync()\nor stream-order synchronization do not\n\nsuffice to establish ordering for tensor map updates, that is, threads in these other thread blocks still need to acquire the tensor map proxy\n\nat the right scope before using the updated tensor map. If there are no intermediate modifications, the fence does not have to be repeated\n\nbefore each\ncp.async.bulk.tensor\ninstruction.\nThe\nfence\nand subsequent use of the tensor map is shown in the following example.\n// Consumer of tensor map in global memory:\n__global__\nvoid\nconsume_tensor_map\n(\nCUtensorMap\n*\ntensor_map\n)\n{\n// Fence acquire tensor map:\nptx\n::\nn\n\n[EXCERPT The remaining data cache serves as an L1 cache and is also used by the texture unit that implements the various addressing and data filtering modes mentioned in\nTexture and Surface Memory\n.\n16.8.2.\nGlobal Memory\n\nGlobal memory behaves the same way as for devices of compute capability 5.x (See\nGlobal Memory\n).\n16.8.3.\nShared Memory\n\nSimilar to the\nNVIDIA Ampere GPU architecture\n, the amount of the unified data cache reserved for shared memory is configurable on a per kernel basis. For the\nNVIDIA H100 Tensor Core GPU architecture\n, the unified data cache has a size of 256 KB for devices of compute capability 9.0. The shared memory capacity can be set to 0, 8, 16, 32, 64, 100, 132, 164, 196 or 228 KB.\nAs with the\nNVIDIA Ampere GPU architecture\n, an application can configure its preferred shared memory capacity, i.e., the\ncarveout\n. Devices of compute capability 9.0 allow a single thread block to address up to 227 KB of shared memory. Kernels relying on shared memory allocations over 48 KB per block are architecture-specific, and must use dynamic shared memory rather than statically sized shared memory arrays. These kernels require an explicit opt-in by using\ncudaFuncSetAttribute()\nto set the\ncudaFuncAttributeMaxDynamicSharedMemorySize\n; see\nShared Memory\nfor the Volta architecture.\nNote that the maximum amount of shared memory per thread block is smaller than the maximum shared memory partition available per SM. The 1 KB of shared memory not made available to a thread block is reserved for system use.\n16.8.4.\nFeatures Accelerating Specialized Computations\n\nThe NVIDIA Hopper GPU architecture includes features to accelerate matrix multiply-accumulate (MMA) computations with:\nasynchronous execution of MMA instructions\nMMA instructions acting on large matrices spanning a warp-group\ndynamic reassignment of register capacity among warp-groups to support even larger matrices, and\noperand matrices accessed directly from shared memory\nThis feature set is only available within the CUDA compilation toolchain through inline PTX.\nIt is strongly recommended that applications utilize this complex feature set through CUDA-X libraries such as cuBLAS, cuDNN, or cuFFT.\nIt is strongly recommended that device kernels utilize this complex feature set through\nCUTLASS\n, a collection of CUDA C++ template abstractions for implementing high-performance matrix-multiplication (GEMM) and related computations at all levels and scales within CUDA.\n16.9.\nCompute Capability 10.0\n\n16.9.1.\nArchitecture\n\nA Streaming Multiprocessor (SM) consists of:\n128 FP32 cores for single-precision arithmetic operations,\n64 FP64 cores for double-precision arithmetic operations,\n64 INT32 cores for integer math,\n4 mixed-precision fifth-generation Tensor Cores supporting\nFP8\ninput type in either\nE4M3\nor\nE5M2\nfor exponent (E) and mantissa (M), half-precision (fp16),\n__nv_bfloat16\n,\ntf32\n, INT8 and double precision (fp64) matrix arithmetic (see\nWarp Matrix Functions\nfor details) with sparsity support,\n16 special function units for single-precision floating-point transcendental functions,\n4 warp schedulers.\nAn SM statically distributes its warps among its schedulers. Then, at every instruction issue time, each scheduler issues one instruction for one of its assigned warps that is ready to execute, if any.\nAn SM has:\na read-only constant cache that is shared by all functional units and speeds up reads from the constant memory space, which resides in device memory,\na unified data cache and shared memory with a total size of 256 KB for devices of compute support hardware-accelerated\n\natomic accesses to CPU-resident memory. This implies that atomic accesses to host memory\n\ndo not have to be emulated with a page fault.\n\nFor these devices, the attribute\ncudaDevAttrHostNativeAtomicSupported\nis set to 1.\n19.2.2.4.\nAtomic accesses & synchronization primitives\n\nCUDA Unified Memory supports all atomic operations available to host and device threads,\n\nenabling all threads to cooperate by concurrently accessing the same shared memory location.\n\nThe\nCUDA C++ standard library\nprovides many heterogeneous synchronization primitives tuned for concurrent use between host and device threads,\n\nincluding\ncuda::atomic\n,\ncuda::atomic_ref\n,\ncuda::barrier\n,\ncuda::semaphore\n, among many others.\nOn systems without\nCPU and GPU page tables: hardware coherency vs. software coherency\n,\n\natomic accesses from the device to file-backed host memory are not supported.\n\nThe following example code is valid on systems with\nCPU and GPU page tables: hardware coherency vs. software coherency\nbut exhibits undefined behavior on other systems:\n#include\n<cuda/atomic>\n#include\n<cstdio>\n#includ", "excerpt_ranges": [[488000, 492000], [500000, 504000], [516000, 524000], [528000, 536000], [588000, 589863], [964000, 968000], [1064000, 1068000]], "range_unit": "zero-based half-open characters in extracted text", "extracted_characters": 1116212, "selected_characters": 33863, "extraction": "UTF-8 text", "selection_reason": "Ranked 4000-character windows by current entry title/tags/questions and algorithm/mechanism keywords;
